Tales of Being Limitless

StickySituation

A complete read of Draft 3: what changed since the last draft, every chapter scored, every character, what every name means, every power and its rules, every theme, motif, and callback, a continuity ledger, theories, craft notes, and a decoder for every chapter title.

Before You Start

This is a full analysis of Tales of Being Limitless: Sticky Situation, in the version you sent as “Sticky Situation 3.” It covers everything from the Prologue through Section 4, Chapter 7 (“Saw Right Through You”), which is new in this draft.

How I read it. I read the whole manuscript start to finish, in order, and wrote notes on every chapter as I went: what happens, every named detail, every line worth quoting, every question it opens. Then I did a second pass with targeted searches. I traced every appearance of the whistle, every “we’re not friends,” every head pat, every eye color, every height, every day of the week, every speed figure, every echoed line. I rebuilt the calendar. I counted words per chapter. I checked every power against the rules its own introduction sets. Finally, I compared this draft against the analysis of your previous draft (it’s in this repository) line by line, so I could tell you exactly what you changed.

What I didn’t do. I didn’t treat Chapter 7 as an ending. The tournament is mid-bracket, Orion and Ailani are about to fight, and half the book’s secrets are still locked. Nothing below faults the book for leaving things open. Open threads go under “Open Threads,” “Theories,” and “Predictions,” where they belong.

A note on quotes and line numbers. Every quote is copied from your text. Where I give a line number (like L1153), it refers to the line in the .txt file you uploaded, so you can jump straight to it.

How this document is organized

  1. The Verdict. The short version, a scorecard, and what the book is about.
  2. What Changed in Draft 3. A revision audit: what you added, cut, fixed, and reworded since the last draft.
  3. The Book by the Numbers. Word counts, the calendar, the class roster, a physical-description ledger, and counts of the book’s habits.
  4. Chapter by Chapter. All 54 chapters plus the Prologue. Each one gets what happens, the details I caught, what it’s doing, what works, what I’d push on, and a score.
  5. Character Dossiers. Every named character, plus the important unnamed ones.
  6. Names, Decoded. What every name in the book means, in the language it comes from. Some of these are jokes I don’t think most readers will catch.
  7. The Powers Compendium. Every enhancement, its costs, its rules, its “tells,” and whether the book keeps its own rules.
  8. Themes. The ideas the book keeps returning to.
  9. Motifs, Symbols, and Callbacks. Recurring objects and images, the full journey of the whistle, and every setup and payoff I could find.
  10. The Relationship Web. Who mirrors whom.
  11. Continuity Ledger. What holds up, what wobbles, and what’s fixed since last draft.
  12. Open Threads. Everything the book has opened and not closed.
  13. Theories and Predictions. Ranked by confidence, with evidence.
  14. Craft Notes. Prose, dialogue, punctuation, pacing, with numbers and specific lines.
  15. The Chapter Title Decoder. Every title, and the pun or irony inside it.
  16. Closing Verdict. The best moments, the best lines, and what I want next.

A note on scores. Scores are relative to this book. A 7 isn’t bad. It means a chapter doing less than the book’s best chapters do. You asked for honesty, so the scores are honest.

1. The Verdict

The short version

Sticky Situation looks like a familiar superhero-school story, and that’s a trap it sets on purpose. There’s one hero academy in the whole world, a lazy prodigy, a speedster rival, a sweet healer, a demon boy, and a televised tournament. Then, over about 107,000 words, the book takes every piece of that setup apart to see what’s rotting underneath. The world’s number one hero sells merch and bets a student’s future for ratings. The tournament is a TV product with a concession stand. The villains offer the boy the attention and mentorship the school never gave him. The healer dies saving him. The protagonist survives only because a monster rebuilt his body, and the rebuild amplifies the worst parts of him, quietly, a little more each chapter.

The engine of the whole thing is Kage Ozul, and he’s the best decision you made. His power is adhesive. His founding wound is a hand that held on to someone’s wrist and then held “air.” He’s an orphan who “stopped unpacking his bag” because “you don’t hang posters if you’re not sure how long the walls are yours.” A boy whose power is attachment, who can’t attach to anyone, whose great failure was failing to hold on: the power, the wound, and the theme are the same thing. Most superhero stories never get their premise that tight.

And then you gave him the best hero name in the genre for the best reason in the genre. He wants to be called Sticky Situation so that the people he saves will laugh, go home, and tell their families, “Can you believe the guy who saved me was named Sticky Situation?” He wants a name “that can turn something horrifying into something funny.” That one speech (1.9) is the book’s soul. Everything since has been a test of whether he can stay that kid.

What the book does best

  • It remembers everything. This book has the best setup-and-payoff discipline I’ve seen in an unpublished draft. The gold glint in Abby’s backpack becomes the whistle. “Another speedster, huh?” becomes Inazuma. The anonymous “Remember this?” maid photo becomes Inazuma again. “Eat breakfast” becomes “I remembered to eat breakfast!” Geneki losing by breaking through the floor becomes the exact rule Ailani uses to beat Lance. Orion’s “That name is terrible, pick anything else” and “I’m not yelling that in a crisis” come back word for word with the speakers swapped. Elias’s “You don’t know my enhancement? That’s unfortunate, but it explains your naive charge” comes back out of Kage’s mouth, aimed at Elias. Even the lipids in the power card come back as a hotel minifridge.
  • Its fights are puzzles with rules. Almost every fight is won by understanding a rule rather than by hitting harder. Lucifer pushes Severine’s half-dome barrier across the floor. Kage beats Elias because excretion counts as an involuntary process, like sweating. Kage beats Abaddon by choking him, not hurting him. Ailani beats Lance because she counted beats and because she rewatched her friend’s loss. And the costs are real: hardening glue draws cold from Kage’s body, so every concrete punch spikes his fever.
  • It builds its cast in mirrors. Two death gods (Anubis and Thanatos) “mentor” Kage and both treat him as an asset. Two ice-and-fire men (Frostbite and Huoyan) are gruff and actually care. Lance is a villain’s son choosing heroism; Kage is a hero student sliding toward villainy. Inazuma and Rebento are both described, by other characters, as Kage with a different power. And now, with Chapter 7, three boys carry a “someone else” inside them: Lucifer has Abaddon, Kage has the biomass, Lance has “King Arthur.”
  • It earns its big emotions. Abby’s death. Orion’s count to three in the shadow dome. The King Kage speech. Lance’s ants and the frog. Kage sticking his tongue out at a scared kid while a ten-foot demon slams him into the floor. Orion raising the whistle alone. These land because the book planted them chapters earlier.
  • It has a point of view about heroism. The book keeps asking whether a hero is someone who saves people or someone people watch. It doesn’t answer cheaply.

What holds it back

  • The prose is louder than it needs to be. There are 2,142 exclamation points in about 107,000 words, about one every 50 words, and 671 fully capitalized words of four letters or more. The announcer-style power introductions earn their volume; that’s a genre device and you use it well. But the exclamation points spread into ordinary conversation and narration, and when everything is loud, the moments that should be loud lose their force.
  • Emotions are often named right after they’re shown. You’ll show a feeling well, then explain it in narration a sentence later. The showing is usually good enough to stand alone.
  • Some sentence-level mechanics still need a pass. You’ve fixed a lot since the last draft (see Section 2), but about 27 dialogue tags still follow a period instead of a comma, and almost all of them are in the second half of the book. There are a couple of tense slips and a “compliment”/“complement” mix-up. All easy.
  • The first round of the tournament is a deliberate anticlimax, and it’s long. Most first-round matches end in seconds by forfeit, fake trip, or ring-out. The book knows this, has characters complain about it, and uses the gaps for character work, and you’ve clearly strengthened those gaps in this draft. It works, but it’s still the slowest stretch of the book.

Scorecard

Category Score Why
Concept and premise 9 One school, a lazy prodigy, a power that is also the wound. Familiar on purpose, then dismantled.
Protagonist 9.5 Kage is funny, wounded, clever, petty, and getting worse in a controlled way.
Supporting cast 8.5 Big and mostly distinct. Lance, Elias, Huoyan, and now Ailani in combat are standouts.
Villains 8.5 Thanatos and Nerezza are frightening in different registers. Frostbite is the book’s best unanswered question.
Worldbuilding 8 Powers have costs and categories. The economics of heroism (funding, merch, sponsors, ratings) is the best part. The UN and Security Accords plots are promising but thin so far.
Plot and structure 8.5 Strong section design and a superb midpoint. The tournament’s first round sags on purpose.
Action 9 Rule-based and inventive. Kage vs. Elias, Kage vs. Abaddon, and Ailani vs. Lance are highlights.
Emotional impact 9 Abby’s death and the dome choice are devastating. Small gestures carry a lot of weight.
Dialogue 7.5 Funny and characterful, sometimes over-explained, and the articulate characters can sound alike.
Prose mechanics 6.5 Better than last draft. The remaining issues are habits, and habits are fixable.
Theme 9.5 Heroism as product versus heroism as comfort. Consistent and rich.
Overall 8.5 A genuinely good story with a real heart and an unusually careful memory, whose sentences haven’t fully caught up to its structure yet.

What the book is really about

Under every chapter is one question: Is a hero someone who saves people, or someone people watch?

Anubis answers watched. He wants cameras in student dorms. He loves Kage’s story because “an orphaned failure turned number one hero just sells itself.” He sells the tournament on TV, counts merch receipts during fights, and gambles Kage’s support on a deal with Lucifer to keep viewers from changing the channel. He tells his students to “win by any means necessary.” When Huoyan, the only adult still searching for Abby, checks his phone for news of her, Anubis says, “That’s not important right now!”

Nerezza, the cruelest villain, answers the same way from the other side. She rewinds Kage’s screams on her phone because “children screaming in pain is next level television.” Put those two side by side, and the book’s thesis is clear: the hero establishment and the most sadistic villain are consuming the same product. So is the crowd. A random spectator yells “KEEP GOING! THIS IS AWESOME!” while two teenagers headbutt each other bloody.

Kage’s first answer was saves. His name exists so that the rescued will laugh. He acts on that philosophy most purely at the worst moment of his life, when he sticks his tongue out at Alex in the stands while Abaddon is smashing him into the floor. Alex laughs. Then Alex cheers. Then his mother cheers. Then the whole stadium does. The book’s best answer to its own question is right there: the person you comfort becomes the person who holds you up.

But Kage also wants to be watched. When the stadium doors open and the crowd cheers, “he wanted this forever.” And the biomass inside him “will amplify your traits, especially the negative ones.” The tragedy the book is building is a boy whose heroism comes from comforting people and whose hunger comes from wanting to be seen, with something inside him feeding the hunger.

Draft 3’s new chapter widens that. Lance, the book’s moral center, turns out to have his own “someone else” inside him, and he named it King Arthur. The boy who knights people and preaches honor goes blind with bloodlust when he fights too long. Kage wants to be king. Lance’s monster is named after one. Lucifer’s is named after the angel of the abyss. The book is now asking a second question alongside the first: What do you do with the part of you that wants to win more than it wants to be good?

That’s a story worth finishing.

2. What Changed in Draft 3

I compared this manuscript against every quote recorded in the analysis of your previous draft (analysis/Sticky_Situation_Deep_Read.md), plus that analysis’s word counts. Here’s what you changed.

The big picture

Part Previous draft Draft 3 Change
Prologue ~1,050 (as recorded) 762 Reworked and tightened
Section 1 24,181 24,180 Essentially unchanged
Section 2 32,861 32,861 Unchanged
Section 3, Ch 1–10 21,089 21,742 +653 words
Section 3, Ch 11–16 15,496 15,496 Unchanged
Section 4, NULL–Ch 6 10,046 10,046 Unchanged
Section 4, Ch 7 — 1,743 New chapter
Total ~104,700 ~106,800 about +2,100

The pattern is clear. You went back to the first round of the tournament, the stretch most likely to sag, and thickened the scenes between fights. Every chapter from 3.1 through 3.10 grew by 29 to 110 words. Chapters 3.11 through 3.16 didn’t change at all. That’s exactly where I’d have told you to spend revision time.

Chapter Before After Added
3.1 And…Begin! 2,181 2,242 +61
3.2 Quick And Painful 3,059 3,088 +29
3.3 Which Kage Is Best? 2,105 2,169 +64
3.4 Forfeit Versus Failure 3,014 3,124 +110
3.5 Just Like You Both 1,144 1,187 +43
3.6 Faster Than Fast 2,408 2,497 +89
3.7 Gone In An Instant 2,385 2,436 +51
3.8 Dinner Needs A Show 1,374 1,461 +87
3.9 A Lighter Crown 1,487 1,531 +44
3.10 Honoring A Snail’s Pace 1,932 2,007 +75

Story-level changes

  1. Thanatos has a new surname. He was “Thanatos Shioya.” Now he introduces himself as “Thanatos Augustus” (L656). That’s a strong change. Augustus means “venerable” or “majestic,” and it was the title of the first Roman emperor. So the villain who recruits a boy who wants to be called King is literally named “Death the Emperor.” It plugs him straight into the book’s royalty motif (King Kage, Prince Kage, Reina the “queen,” Lance’s knighting, Lance’s “King Arthur”).
  2. Kage’s disguise contacts are now yellow. They used to be gray. Frostbite now hands him “a pair of yellow contacts” (L1379). Abby is the only character whose natural eye color is yellow (L17). So when Kage goes out as Frostbite’s operative, he’s literally wearing the dead girl’s eye color. If that’s deliberate, it’s chilling and beautiful. If it isn’t, keep it anyway.
  3. The maid-photo payoff is sharper. Inazuma now says, “I sent him that ‘Remember this?’ pic of him in a maid outfit” (L2007), quoting the caption from 1.5 (L167) exactly. The setup and payoff now click audibly.
  4. Cheyenne quotes the King speech word for word. In 3.9, she now recites “‘People will call me King Kage one day. That’s not arrogance, by the way. It’s a spoiler.’” (L2609), which matches 1.11 (L612) exactly. In the previous draft it was a paraphrase. A character remembering someone’s words perfectly is a form of respect, and now the text shows it.
  5. Lucifer calls Reina “Cruz” in the backroom deal. “If I use my enhancement on Cruz, my chances of beating her plummet” (L2549). He used to say “Reina.” He calls her Cruz everywhere else, so this is a consistency fix.
  6. Huoyan’s rebuttal of Anubis’s orphan speech is stronger. Huoyan now says, “Plus, you know that line about every kid having parents cheering isn’t completely true. If that were really your reason, you’d be rooting for every kid out there with no one in the stands or watching on T.V.” (L1786). That’s a much better hit than the old version, because it exposes Anubis’s sympathy as selective.
  7. Orion’s reaction to apologizing is sharper. His interior line now names the slur: “He was the one Inazuma had called a ‘big black dolt,’ but now he was the one apologizing? Whatever calm he’d scraped together started slipping again” (L2251). Followed by “His hand balled into a fist at his side before he made it let go” (L2253). That makes the injustice concrete and sets up his loss of control against Inazuma.
  8. Severine gets a moment of loneliness. In her meltdown, “for a moment that rage quieted enough for something somber to bleed through” (L1845). One clause, and it turns a comic tantrum into a person.
  9. Cheyenne’s early start has a rules justification. “Technically, the rules only said they could start as soon as Gene said so, and Gene had definitely said ‘one.’” (L2185). That’s better than the old “there was no rule,” because it uses Gene’s exact wording, and it’s the same logic Kage uses later.
  10. Anubis’s “shoot the villain” line is cleaner. “If I were the one who needed rescuing, I wouldn’t care if you needed to shoot the villain” (L2375).
  11. Kage’s half-truth to Lance is more specific. “I was the only one who fought a semi-final that hadn’t used their power all day” (L2998).
  12. Small beats were added around the whistle. “At the word ‘support,’ his hand instinctively clutched Abby’s whistle” (L2109). Kage “caught himself and tried to tuck it back under his collar, but Cheyenne clocked it quickly” (L2611). Ailani: “You told me that whistle was hers, right? I saw him clutch it when yelling at Lance” (L2127). These tie the whistle into his outbursts so readers can track his grief through the object.
  13. The Prologue was reworked. Its last line used to be a direct question (“Is he becoming the kind of hero who saves people…?”). Now it’s reported thought: “every time he catches his reflection, he wonders if he’s becoming the kind of hero who saves people, or someone just desperate not to lose somebody like that again.” It’s quieter, and it fits the Prologue’s controlled voice better.
  14. Section 4, Chapter 7 is new. It’s a wordless duel between Ailani and Lance, and it reveals Lance’s “limit.” More on it below.

Mechanical fixes

  • Dialogue tags. The previous analysis counted about 54 places where a dialogue tag followed a period ("No, you won't." Anubis answered). I count about 27 now, so you fixed roughly half. 24 of the 27 that remain are in Section 3, Chapter 11 or later. It looks like you revised from the front and haven’t reached the back yet. The new Chapter 7 has none.
  • Curly quotes. The old draft had about 30 curly apostrophes and 3 curly quote marks mixed in with straight ones. Draft 3 has zero. Clean.
  • Hyphens and commas. “different-colored,” “razor-sharp,” “low-blowing,” “small-scale,” “You can do it, sweetie!!!,” “didn’t respond to or acknowledge anyone.” All fixed.
  • A word swap that matters. “it didn’t take long to find that obvious hair” (L772) replaced “striking hair.” “Obvious” is funnier, because it’s Orion’s annoyance talking.

What didn’t change (and still could)

These were flagged last time and are still in the text. None are serious.

  • “No one else knew Kage called Orion that” (L1599). Abby and Zulfiya both heard him say “Hermes” in the nurse’s office (2.2). Zulfiya even reacts to it in 2.14. The line works as Zulfiya’s thought, but the narration states it as fact.
  • How Lucifer “got wind” that Kage and Ailani were talking again (L1031). They reconciled in a hidden room on a Friday. By Monday he’d told her about Reina. Who told him?
  • Gene’s reason for switching the match order (3.11). Huoyan asks, “What makes this different?” and she doesn’t answer. If that’s characterization (Gene is less principled than she claims), it works. If it’s meant to be a real rescue, it needs one more step.
  • Severine is set up as speaking without contractions (“I do not date fools”) but uses them often.
  • C.O.R.E. calls Orion “a model student with no filed record of misconduct” (L123) the day after Huoyan warned him.

Net verdict on the revision: you listened, and you revised in the right places. The first round reads better. The callbacks are tighter. The surname change and the yellow contacts both add meaning. Now finish the punctuation pass from 3.11 to the end.

3. The Book by the Numbers

Size and shape

The manuscript runs about 106,800 words of story (107,128 counting headings) across a Prologue, four Sections, and 54 numbered chapters. Section 4 opens with a “Chapter NULL” before its Chapter 1.

Section Chapters Words Average per chapter Share of book
Prologue 1 762 — 0.7%
Section 1: Orion Vance 15 24,180 1,612 22.6%
Section 2: Abby Neosame 15 32,861 2,191 30.8%
Section 3: Elite Tournament 16 37,238 2,327 34.9%
Section 4: Ailani Shoma (so far) 8 11,789 1,474 11.0%

Two things stand out. First, the chapters get longer as the stakes rise, from about 1,600 words in Section 1 to about 2,300 in Section 3. Second, the book is already longer than most debut novels, and the tournament still has at least three matches left. If you’re planning for publication, you’re likely looking at a two-book split. (More on that in Craft Notes.)

The four longest chapters are Abby’s death (2.6), the dome and the rebuild (2.7), Kage vs. Elias (3.11), and Kage vs. Abaddon (3.16). Those are the four most important chapters in the book. The shortest (2.5, 1.3, 4.5, 4.0) are pivots and breathers. Your instinct for where to spend words is good.

Two notes. Abby has only 176 mentions, fewer than Lance, yet her section and her death are the emotional center of the book. That’s efficient writing: she’s a small presence with an enormous footprint. And Ailani, whose section we’re now in, has only been a fighter for one chapter. Section 4 is clearly where she gets her due.

The calendar

I rebuilt the timeline from every time reference in the text. It’s remarkably consistent.

When What happens Where
Before Year 1 Kage bounces between foster homes in Japan; knows Inazuma from middle school Prologue, 3.3
Year 1, week 2 Abby confronts Lucifer about what’s draining him 2.1
Year 1 Kage sleeps through class, works at the maid café, meets Ailani over honey toast, dates her 1.5, 4.1
Late Year 1 They break up (“months ago”); Kage dates nine women 1.3
Start of Year 2 The convenience store; someone dies under the awning Prologue, 1.11
Shortly after The field spar; Anubis calls them “brilliant” 1.1–1.2
The next day Ailani slams the door; Orion meets Rhatella 1.3–1.4
Around then Max-settings robonoids; “number one supporter” 1.5
~2 months later, a Saturday The race across the city 1.6
A couple of days later (Monday) Huoyan’s scolding; Severine 1.7–1.8
~2 months after that Rhatella’s lab; Thursday class; the collapse (“four months”) 1.9–1.11
That night / next night / next morning Thanatos; Frostbite; Anubis’s office 1.12–1.14
A few weeks later (a Thursday) Schoolyard fight; Kage’s first win 1.15

The weekend Texting Ailani; Sunday goes cold 2.4
Monday Ailani’s door; Lucifer’s door; Abby at Lucifer’s door 2.4–2.5
Monday night The patrol; Abby dies; Kage is broken and rebuilt 2.6–2.7
Tuesday “The day of the extremes” for Orion 2.8
Wednesday Orion tells Ailani that Kage is dead 2.8
Thursday Huoyan vs. Anubis; Kage leaves the bathroom; the makeover; the infiltration; Abaddon 2.9–2.12
Saturday Tournament day one 2.13–3.12
Saturday night The hotel; Kage and Lance 3.12–3.13
Sunday Tournament day two (where the book is now) 3.14–4.7

Checks that pass:

  • “Two months” (1.6) plus “two more months” (1.9) equals Huoyan’s “four months” (1.11).
  • Huoyan’s “only been what…six months?” (3.3) matches Inazuma’s “past six months” (3.6).
  • Huoyan says Kage and Abby have been missing “since Tuesday… three days now” (2.9). Tuesday, Wednesday, Thursday is three days, and Anubis later confirms that Kage was in the building on Thursday (2.15).
  • Orion says in 2.2, “When I spoke to Huoyan yesterday,” which places the schoolyard fight the day before the nurse’s office. Huoyan’s “Enjoy your weekend… You start Monday!” makes the nurse’s office a Friday. So the fight was a Thursday.
  • Nerezza says, “it’s only been a week since I’ve heard that noise” (4.0). Monday night to Sunday is six days.
  • Kage says, “it’s only been a little over a week since we last fought” (4.6). Thursday to the second Sunday is ten days.
  • Rebento says Lance “just met Kage yesterday” (4.6). They met properly on Saturday’s walk.

That’s careful work. The calendar holds.

The class roster

The Prologue says only twenty applicants are selected. These are the second-years I can place:

# Student Power In the tournament?
1 Rebento Azucre Sweet-Toothed Surprise Yes (semifinalist)
2 Cheyenne Bandalore Unrevealed Yes (R1 loss)
3 Reina Cruz Heat Charge Yes (semifinalist)
4 Lance Faisceau Avalon’s Legacy Yes (semifinalist)
5 Geneki Gardner Unnamed (green-energy strength) Yes (R1 loss)
6 Rhatella Gardner Wireplay No
7 Alexander Lund Unrevealed Yes (R1 loss)
8 Elias Minkowski Chronal Inertia Yes (semifinalist)
9 Youseff Nasser Unrevealed Yes (R1 “loss”)
10 Severine Neant Repulse-Redux Yes (R1 loss)
11 Abby Neosame Eyes of Divinity No (missing, presumed dead)
12 Kage Ozul Adhesive Manipulation Yes (grand finalist)
13 Volkan Salyangozu Volcano Snail Yes (R1 loss)
14 Zulfiya Shifo Mending Mane Yes (R1 forfeit)
15 Ailani Shoma Crystal Release Yes (half-finalist)
16 Inazuma Sokudo Lightning Fast Yes (R1 loss)
17 Lucifer Tulivalo Hellspawn Yes (half-finalist)
18 Orion Vance Celestial Velocity Yes (half-finalist)

That’s 18. Kelly Iasacht’s son, who “didn’t end up showing up,” is probably the 19th. That leaves one classmate we’ve never met, if the class is still twenty. It could be nothing. It could also be the most useful empty chair in the book.

The tournament has 16 slots: Anubis says “fifteen wonderful students ready and enrolled,” plus the slot he “saved” for Kage. So the four non-participants are Rhatella, Abby, Kelly’s son, and the mystery twentieth.

Rhatella’s alphabet. Rhatella builds gear “in alphabetical order by last name” and says she’s on “O” (1.4). Every piece of Rhatella gear in the tournament belongs to a student earlier in the alphabet: Azucre’s gumball machines, Cruz’s energy pack, Faisceau’s hilts (both of them), Minkowski’s glasses. Nobody after “O” has her gear, not Lucifer (T), not Orion (V), and not Kage himself (O), whose gear she’s still fighting with because “as a conductor, trying to make an item for a human insulator is the WORST!” That’s the kind of continuity most writers would never think to keep.

One wrinkle: the class may not be a single fixed group of twenty. Kage wonders “why he was in the top class” and whether he’d be “moved to a lower class” (1.14). Ailani threatens to “switch classes” (1.3). First-year Kage tells Ailani “you’re in the top class like me” (4.1). So the hero program seems to have tiers, and “general studies” is separate again. If twenty are selected per year and split into classes, say so once; otherwise, readers will try to make “twenty” and “top class” fit together.

Every height, eye color, and distinguishing feature the book gives. Useful for illustrators, and for keeping yourself consistent.

Everything here is consistent across the book. The only soft spot is Kage’s eyes in 2.14. He has yellow contacts for patrol, and Ailani stares “deep into his blue eyes” at the stadium. That’s fine, since he says the disguise is for “while out on patrol.” One clause showing him take the contacts out before coming back would close it completely.

4. Chapter by Chapter

Each chapter gets a meta line (length, setting, timing), then: What happens, Details I caught (the small stuff, with quotes), What it’s doing, What works, What I’d push on, and a score.

Prologue and Section 1: “Orion Vance”

Prologue

762 words. Omniscient narrator, present and past tense. Before and at the start of Kage’s second year.

What happens. The narrator lays out the world: everyone is born with an enhancement, enhancements decide your life, heroes get cameras and sponsors, villains get erased. One specialized hero program exists, attached to a “seemingly normal” American high school, and “no one’s sure why the United Nations only wants one program in one school.” Kage Ozul, a foster kid from Japan, joined it for a bed, three meals, and a door that locks. He coasted through his first year on purpose. Then one night, a villain blew out the windows of his favorite convenience store. Kage grabbed someone’s wrist, the awning came down, and “his fingers were holding air.” Since then, he trains until his hands split.

Details I caught.

  • “Some freak mutation dressed up as destiny.” The narrator is cynical about the whole system from the first paragraph.
  • “In many young minds, it’s the same game…they just have different costumes.” This is the book’s thesis in one line: hero and villain might be a costume choice. Everything with Thanatos later grows out of this sentence.
  • “Agencies chase ratings more than rescues.” That’s Anubis, described before we meet him.
  • “Kids get scouted young, polished into smiling weapons with brand logos on their chests.” This comes back in 1.12 as “weapons who smiled because it was good for the branding,” at the exact moment Kage shakes the villain’s hand. And it comes back again in 4.2, when Kage and Lucifer are literally dressed in hero-program merchandise because their clothes were burned off.
  • “Their single polite little factory for ‘hope.’” The scare quotes around “hope” tell you how the narrator feels.
  • “It was just a bed that didn’t move every month.” Kage’s motive, stated in one image.
  • “Sharp enough to ace anything, tired enough not to bother.” This line becomes important later. When Kage rattles off five authentic epithets of Hermes in 1.15, or explains “fire-stop caulk” in 3.15, it’s the “sharp enough to ace anything” half finally showing up.
  • “He wears the air of someone who not only doesn’t care, but would tape his laziness and study that to get even lazier.” The first tape joke, in the Prologue.
  • “You don’t hang posters if you’re not sure how long the walls are yours.” The best sentence in the Prologue, and one of the best in the book.
  • “The entrance exam nearly broke him. A test with thousands of applicants. And yet, only twenty get selected.” That’s the class-size number the roster has to answer to.
  • “An enhancement he refused to explain.” When people ask, “he just shrugs. Says, ‘Doesn’t matter.’” This is still an open thread. His power turns out to be adhesive, which isn’t embarrassing enough to explain a refusal. Either there’s more to it (see Theories), or it was shame.
  • “Teachers called it potential, and they said it like a prayer.” “Potential” becomes a curse word in this book. Thanatos promises to end his status as “potential man.” Frostbite says Thanatos “doesn’t see your full potential,” and Kage groans, “You’re another one of those.”
  • “Then one night, the world reminded him what happens to ghosts.” Kage’s first-year self is a ghost. Then he becomes the haunted one.
  • The windows “went white and burst outward.” White. Not orange, not bright. That’s what frost does to glass. Hold on to that.
  • “His hand closed around a wrist… When the dust cleared, his fingers were holding air.” The central image of the book.
  • “Adhering to every rule—well, most of the time.” Another adhesive pun.
  • The last line: “every time he catches his reflection, he wonders if he’s becoming the kind of hero who saves people, or someone just desperate not to lose somebody like that again.” That’s the book’s other thesis question, and it’s the right one. Saving people and refusing to lose people sound like the same thing. They aren’t.

What it’s doing. It gives you the world’s politics, the protagonist’s wound, and the book’s two questions (hero vs. watched; saving vs. not-losing) before a single line of dialogue.

What works. The voice is literary, controlled, and concrete. The imagery is all about walls, hands, ghosts, and reflections, and every one of those images returns. The reworked final line (now reported thought instead of a direct question) is better than the old version. It’s quieter, and it ends the Prologue looking into a mirror, which is where the book keeps returning (the mirror room, Ailani’s “that mirror definitely blushes”).

What I’d push on. The jump from this voice to Chapter 1’s bright, exclamatory, anime-style register is abrupt. Both voices are good. The best chapters later (2.6, 2.7, 3.16) blend them. You could either bring a little of the Prologue’s control into Chapter 1, or give the Prologue one beat of humor so the shift doesn’t feel like a different book. Also, “No one’s sure why the United Nations only wants one program” is a big mystery to plant this early. It’s been 106,000 words. Readers will want at least a crumb soon. (The Security Accords in 4.0 are a good start.)

Score 8.5/10

1.1 Nice To Meet You

1,177 words. The training field, shortly after “that night.”

What happens. Kage is punching a heavy bag until his knuckles split. Orion, a classmate he barely knows, checks on him and mentions the store. Abby, a tiny girl with a double halo, offers water from her dolphin backpack and heals his hands. Kage, thrilled by her healing, proposes a no-holds-barred fight with Orion, since Abby can fix them both afterward. Abby counts them down.

Details I caught.

  • The opening line: “Kage Ozul used to come to the training field to hit on chicks, but now it was where he made his hands bleed.” The whole character arc, before and after, in one sentence.
  • “All the alternate universes where his grip held.” Grip again.
  • Orion: “I think we’re in the same class but…” A class of twenty, more than a year in, and they barely know each other. That’s how invisible first-year Kage made himself.
  • Orion already knows: “You were the one at that store, right?” So the incident was public. People know Kage is “the kid who froze,” which is exactly how he describes his reputation in 1.11.
  • Orion’s line “happens to more heroes than you think” sounds like experience. Has Orion lost someone? He later says Kage “was the closest person to me who’s ever passed on” (2.13), so probably not. It reads instead as something he was taught. Lance will say the same thing later: “All heroes freeze, Kage.”
  • Abby’s first words to Kage: “I’m Abby, and your hands?” Her first instinct is the injury, not the introduction.
  • Her first full sentence to him: “It’s okay, Kage. Everything is going to be alright.” Those are, almost exactly, her last words (2.6). The narration points it out there. This is where it starts.
  • The first power card: “The girl is Abby Neosame! Her enhancement is Eyes of Divinity!” She can “see through anyone.” She heals. She has wings. “Some are worried she may actually have fallen from heaven.” That last sentence is a seed I’ll come back to.
  • Abby’s limits: the wounded must be alive, “it doesn’t work on me,” and she has to act fast when it’s serious. Every one of those limits matters in 2.6.
  • “Also, fights need a permit; I’m not breaking rules.” Her stern voice, undercut by “her small stature and cute voice.”
  • Kage’s speech tics appear right away: “s-s-s-suck,” “Yes, yes, yes!” His “yapping started speeding up.” He talks faster when excited. Later he repeats “You said ‘yet’!” eight times in a row.
  • “Kage’s crooked grin returned for the first time since that night.” The book tracks his smile like a vital sign.
  • Orion asks what his enhancement is. “Kage dodged the question.” Consistent with the Prologue.
  • Abby “was already scanning both of them for injuries” before the fight starts. She’s going to be a medic whether they listen or not.

What it’s doing. It introduces the core trio in the order they matter to Kage: the rival who sees him, then the supporter who heals him.

What works. Orion’s gentleness is a surprise. He’s not introduced as a rival; he’s introduced as the only person who checked on Kage. That makes their later rivalry warmer. Abby’s introduction is efficient: three lines and you know her completely, which is what makes her death land.

What I’d push on. The power card arrives in the middle of a scene, in a much louder voice than the narration around it. That’s the device, and it’s fine. But Abby’s card ends with three exclamation sentences in a row that all restate “she’s like an angel.” Cut one. Also, Kage asking for a fight “to the brink of death” two lines after meeting a healer is funny, but it would land harder with one beat of interiority: he wants to hurt something, and now he’s found a way to do it without consequences.

Score 8/10

1.2 Birth Of A Rivalry

1,855 words. The training field, same scene.

What happens. The spar. Kage opens with tape, Orion dodges with an “Aether Step.” Kage spins like a top spraying glue, gets kicked in the sternum, gets back up. Huoyan Niao (Hellfeather, their teacher) and Khaled Nasser (Anubis, the number one hero) arrive; Huoyan wants to stop it, Anubis says let it finish. Kage anchors himself with hardened glue, eats Orion’s full-power “Divine Relay” kick, grabs Orion’s leg, and slams them both into the ground before passing out. Abby heals them. Anubis calls them “brilliant,” says “My agency will be watching,” and leaves. Huoyan threatens essays.

Details I caught.

  • Kage’s card: Adhesive Manipulation. He consumes lipids, excretes adhesives “through his nails,” and “if he hasn’t eaten in a while, then there’s no gas in the tank.” Hardening “requires the cold, and that cold must be drawn from his body!” Overuse causes “fever and exhaustion.” Every one of these rules is used later. The fever climbs from 102°F to 105°F in this chapter, and to 110°F by 2.2. The cold-for-hardening rule is why his temperature spikes while he refills his concrete gauntlets against Abaddon in 3.16.
  • Orion’s card: Celestial Velocity. Gold and violet energy, “comet trails,” time “nearly stands still,” up to “two hundred miles per hour.” Costs: “his body overheats, his joints strain, and a single misstep could tear him apart.” Named techniques: Aether Step, Divine Relay (stored kinetic energy into a strike), and Zephyr Mode. The 200 mph cap matters: he breaks it at 300 mph to save Abby (2.6).
  • “Your power is some tape? That’s what was so worth hiding?” Orion asks the question the reader is asking.
  • Kage: “Another speedster, huh? Ugh, I hate you fast types.” That “another” pays off 2,000 lines later, when Inazuma Sokudo turns out to be a speedster Kage has known since middle school.
  • “My power is made to slow things down!” Kage vs. Orion is slow vs. fast, sticky vs. slippery. Orion’s guilty pleasure, we learn two chapters later, is walking slowly.
  • Abby steps back because “she didn’t want that stuff on her sweater!” A small comic beat that keeps the fight from getting grim.
  • Orion: “Speed isn’t just running straight. It’s perception and timing.” This becomes his catchphrase, and Kage turns it into a nickname (“Mr. Perception-And-Timing”), and Orion turns it into an insult against Inazuma (“Your perception of me is off… your timing is predictable”).
  • Kage: “You thought the dude who messed up being a hero would be easy pickings, huh?!” Nobody said that. Kage hears it anyway.
  • Glue jets “from his toenails.” That’s why he throws his shoes off before every serious fight later (1.8, 2.6, 2.7). Consistent across the book.
  • Huoyan and Anubis are introduced through Abby’s eyes: “one with pale skin and bright white hair, the other with gray hair and sunglasses.”
  • Huoyan: “I have morons for students.” And: “They’re setting a terrible example! That reflects on me!” His first worry is image. He’ll spend the rest of the book proving that isn’t who he is.
  • Anubis: “If they’re doing this out of competition, not malice, then it’s a spar.” He’s not wrong. He’s also not the one who’d be blamed.
  • “This time, he made sure his grip held.” The Prologue’s wound, healed in miniature, in his first real fight. It’s the single best callback in Section 1.
  • Abby can see through her own hands: she “covered her eyes. Covering didn’t matter; she could see the effort spiked his body temperature.”
  • Abby’s diagnosis: Orion has “vibration fatigue and inertia trauma.” Kage is at risk of heatstroke. The medical vocabulary makes her competent, not just sweet.
  • Anubis: “C’mon, Hon, admit it.” He calls Huoyan “Hon” for the whole book. It’s short for Huoyan. It’s also short for “honey.” Section 2 confirms they have history.
  • Huoyan: “seeing Ozul put in effort for once…maybe this could be helpful.” He’s been watching Kage longer than he admits.
  • Anubis: “You two were brilliant.” Kage: “Brilliant? That word had never been used to describe anything Kage had done before.” This one word drives Kage for the rest of Section 1.
  • “He turned as his own enhancement seemed to activate. ‘My agency will be watching.’” We never see what activates. Anubis’s power is still unrevealed. “Watching” is the most important word in his vocabulary.
  • Huoyan’s threat: “essays on hero history.” He later realizes reports won’t work: “Ozul just wouldn’t do it, and Vance’s hands move too fast.”
  • Kage: “Next time, I’m gonna blow your ass up with glue.” Orion, grinning: “Sure. I look forward to it.” The rivalry is born, and it’s affectionate from the first minute.

What it’s doing. It establishes both powers and their costs through a fight, introduces the two most important adults, and gives Kage his first reason to try: being seen by the number one hero.

What works. The fight is genuinely inventive for a first fight. Kage’s anchor-and-grab is a strategy, not a power-up, and it costs him (a blood cough, a 105° fever). It’s also the moment where his power, his trauma, and his character fuse: the boy who couldn’t hold on chooses to hold on even when it nearly kills him. The adults are drawn sharply in very few lines.

What I’d push on. The power cards are long, and Orion’s ends with “not even the gods could outrun his light!”, which is a lot of praise for a character we’ve just met. Consider trimming each card to its rules and one flourish. And Anubis’s “his own enhancement seemed to activate” is so vague that readers might think it’s a mistake. Give it one concrete sensory detail (a hum, a flicker in his sunglasses, the air shifting), and you’ll make the mystery feel intentional.

Score 9/10

1.3 A Beautiful Past

953 words. Training room one, the next day.

What happens. Kage, newly determined to impress Anubis, goes to the training room for the first time ever. He’s drawn to a girl on a treadmill, who turns out to be his ex, Ailani Shoma. She tries to leave. He blocks the door, hugs her from behind, and begs for another chance. A sigil flares on her hand and fades. She tells him they broke up months ago, that he’s dated at least eight women since, and that she’ll switch classes if he talks to her again. She slams the door.

Details I caught.

  • “You gotta step up your game when the number one hero is watching you!” Kage’s motivation is now visibility. One word from Anubis did that.
  • Training room one is described in loving detail: robot sparring on the right, equipment on the left, “Omni Shoulder Press and Dialed Motion systems.” “Staring him in the face like a list of things he’d been avoiding.” That’s a lovely line.
  • “It was a place he should’ve been to at least a hundred times by now… And now, that stung a little.” The first time the book lets Kage regret his first year in narration.
  • He ogles her body before he sees her face. The book doesn’t flatter him.
  • Ailani is “a Latina, Black, and Japanese mixed woman.” Her heritage shows up later in her Spanish curses (HIJO DE PUTA, PENDEJO, IMBÉCIL, CRETINO, CÁLLENSE).
  • “Kage could almost imagine the smell of fresh-baked bread and honey.” That’s a planted seed. Section 4, Chapter 1 is called “Honey Toast,” and it’s the story of their first date at a bakery.
  • “She’d gotten pretty used to ignoring and avoiding him… like she’d rehearsed this a hundred times in her head.” She’s been doing this for months.
  • “Hey, babe! How’s it hanging?! Y’know I take all forms of communication. Call, DM, text, email, smoke signal, letter in a bottle..?” Funny and sad.
  • She covers her face, but “Kage could see the slightest smile. She wiped it off almost instantly.” She still likes him. She’s fighting it.
  • The hug from behind, “clinging to her like he could hold on to the version of them that was already gone.” Clinging. Holding on. The power is the personality.
  • “Ailani stiffened as a sigil appeared on her hand. She exhaled, the sigil disappeared.” She almost used her power on him and chose not to. We don’t know her power yet. This is how we learn its tell.
  • “Kage could feel the emptiness in his hands again.” The hands again.
  • “I know you’ve dated at least eight women in that time frame.” “Actually…it was nine.” The funniest line in the chapter and one of the most damning.
  • “I’m just trying to be with someone ’cause I adore you.” He facepalms at himself. He knows that sentence makes no sense.
  • “Just like how we met, remember?” He says they met sparring. Section 4, Chapter 1 confirms it: she beat him with one crystal to the stomach.
  • Ailani: “What’s done is done, and what happened, happened.” That’s the same euphemism Kage uses for the store (“since…what happened…happened”). She’s talking about the breakup, but the phrase ties her loss to his.
  • The threat to “switch classes.” This implies more than one class, which complicates the Prologue’s “only twenty get selected.” See the roster note.
  • The ending: “leaving him alone with the hum of machines and everything he hadn’t fixed yet.” The first of many doors Ailani shuts on him.

What it’s doing. It introduces Ailani as the person who knew Kage before he changed, and shows that his new effort doesn’t erase his old patterns.

What works. It’s short, sharp, and painful. The title is perfectly ironic. Ailani’s half-smile and rehearsed exit tell you everything about where she is without anyone saying it.

What I’d push on. The hug. Ailani has told him to move, then pushed past him, and he hugs her from behind anyway. The narration frames it as longing, and that’s honest to Kage, but the scene doesn’t show any awareness that this crosses a line. The book later becomes very interested in consent and bodies (Zulfiya’s “he can’t be forced,” Frostbite’s “without consent”). It would strengthen that thread if Kage, at some point, realized this hug was part of what Ailani meant. Even a line in 2.3 (“I’m sorry about grabbing you”) would do it.

Score 7.5/10

1.4 Rhat Tech

1,401 words. Rhatella’s dorm, the same day.

What happens. Orion, enjoying a rare slow walk, finds a dorm door leaking blue light. Inside is a building-sized lab run by an AI, C.O.R.E., which tries to take his blood, lists its three objectives, and lets him meet its creator: Rhatella Gardner, a four-foot-eight inventor with wires in her head and rat ears. She’s building support gear for everyone in alphabetical order and is stuck on Kage. Orion turns down gear, asks if she has friends, and offers to be one. They fist-bump.

Details I caught.

  • “A slow walk through the halls was a guilty pleasure he barely got to enjoy.” The fastest boy in school loves going slow. That’s the best small character detail in Section 1. And it pays off backwards in 3.5, when Inazuma sneers, “A speedster should never make things go slower.”
  • The dorm is “much, much bigger on the inside… like five electrical factories mashed into one.” A TARDIS joke, and it raises a fun question: how does a student have a factory-sized dorm? (Gene, the Elite Program’s creator, is Rhatella’s mother.)
  • “Yesterday a huge spar, and today this?” That dates the chapter to the day after 1.2.
  • The syringe. C.O.R.E. tries to draw Orion’s blood. He slaps it away. Two months later, it succeeds with Kage (1.9). That blood sample is from before the biomass. Nobody has mentioned it since.
  • C.O.R.E.: “You do not wish to beg my creator for advice or support gear? You are not here to scold her for her creations? That is…new.” People only visit Rhatella to use her or yell at her.
  • C.O.R.E.’s three objectives: respond to stimuli; protect its creator “whether from physical or psychological distress”; protect itself and the facility. It’s a lopsided version of Asimov’s three laws: the robot is loyal to one person, not humanity.
  • “I am supportive of her, not you.” Supportive. That word again. It’s everywhere in this book.
  • C.O.R.E.: “a model student with no filed record of misconduct.” Orion was warned by Huoyan yesterday. Either warnings aren’t filed, or C.O.R.E.’s data lags. Tiny wobble.
  • “If at any point I detect ill intent, I will not hesitate to terminate you.” It’s funny, and it’s also the kind of line that becomes a threat later.
  • Rhatella’s look: “a brown face marked by a cleft lip and metallic wires sprouting from her head. Swirling goggles… cute little rat-like ears.”
  • “I go in alphabetical order by last name, and you’re…‘V’? Yeah, you gotta wait, bub!”
  • “I’m on ‘O’ right now, but GOD, this Ozul kid is driving me crazy! As a conductor, trying to make an item for a human insulator is the WORST!” Kage is a human insulator. Fat insulates. But it’s also a strange thing to emphasize if it’s just about fat. Keep it in mind.
  • Her card: Wireplay. She grows and controls cables, slips them into machines, “electricity races harmlessly across her skin,” and “some even believe she might one day manipulate solid metal itself!” That last line is a promise.
  • “Every gadget on the shelves carried the same tiny stamp: a little cartoon of Rhatella’s own face.” Branding again. Kage sees that stamp on Elias’s glasses in 3.11 and hesitates before breaking them.
  • “It’s kinda the only way people talk to me… as long as I’m useful, right?” Rhatella believes her value is her usefulness. Abby believes her purpose is helping. Zulfiya will forfeit her future to “support” Kage. The book is full of girls who think they’re worth what they give.
  • Orion: “I like doing things on my own. That’s the best way to improve, right?” That’s Orion’s flaw, stated plainly. Two chapters later Kage will say nearly the same thing (“I’m gonna be able to handle it all on my own!”).
  • “You shouldn’t hang out with people who only talk to you for your work. Do you have any friends?”
  • “Of course! I mean…kind of…”
  • He crouches “to meet her eye level,” ruffles her hair (a spark jolts him), and “adjust[s] her slipping goggles.” This is the first head-touch in a book full of them.
  • “You’re officially my longest conversation with a classmate…ever!”
  • “Friends?” she says, raising her fist. “Yeah. Friends.” The word Orion and Kage will refuse to say about each other for the entire book, Orion says easily here.

What it’s doing. It gives Orion a life outside Kage, introduces a lonely genius whose gear will quietly shape the whole tournament, and plants the blood sample.

What works. C.O.R.E. has real personality on very little page time. Rhatella’s loneliness is set up gently and pays off later (her “Lord knows he wouldn’t take my heart!” in 4.0). Orion is at his best here: kind, observant, and self-contained.

What I’d push on. Rhatella’s card says she might “one day manipulate solid metal itself.” That’s a big promise, and she’s been offstage since Section 2. If she’s going to matter later (and she should), give her a scene soon. The broken gear from the tournament is the perfect excuse.

Score 8/10

1.5 Stronger Than Strong

1,722 words. Training room three.

What happens. Avoiding Ailani, Kage hits max settings on the robot trainers and gets dogpiled. Abby stops the robots, counts his injuries (27 cuts, 14 bruises), and heals him. A text arrives from “someone he knew”: a photo of Kage in a maid outfit, captioned “Remember this?” Abby worked at a maid café too. Kage goes back to max settings over and over, refusing healing. He vows to get stronger than Orion, “that glasses wearer,” “that horned asshole,” Huoyan, and Anubis. Abby, watching, whispers that she’ll be his “number one supporter.”

Details I caught.

  • Kage avoids room one “to respect Ailani’s wishes.” That’s growth, and it’s quiet.
  • Lifting weights “wasn’t training; it was maintenance.” He wants meaning, not reps.
  • “MAX SETTINGS.” “Perfect.” A two-word paragraph. Great comic timing.
  • “Easy-peasy! This is max? Psh, I got this shit in the b-” Then thirty more robots. The cutoff is perfectly placed.
  • The robots obey a girl’s voice: “STOP STOP!!!” Abby shouts. The voice modules listen.
  • “Exactly twenty-seven cuts and fourteen bruises.” Her precision is part of her power. Zulfiya later does the same thing (“Four hours. No, four hours and twenty minutes.”).
  • “Kage saw something gold sparkle down at the bottom” of the dolphin backpack. That’s the whistle, handed over in 2.2. A plant 800 lines early.
  • “I’m training to be a support hero!… I really don’t like seeing people in pain…”
  • The photo: “Remember this?” Kage in a black-and-white maid outfit, long stockings, black heels. The sender is “someone he knew.” In 3.3, Inazuma reveals he sent it, and in Draft 3 he quotes the caption.
  • “I AM NOT CUTE!”
  • “Kage sighed the heaviest sigh to ever have been sighed.” Good comic narration.
  • Abby: “No way! I worked at a maid cafe too!” Same café? The book never says. It would be a nice later discovery.
  • Kage: “The pay was good, and I needed the money.” An orphan in a foreign country needed money. That’s not a joke.
  • “They asked me to do it again this year, but I turned it down.” He’s choosing training over income. That’s a real sacrifice for him.
  • “You think Orion is doing tutorial stages right now? No.” He’s measuring himself against Orion now, not Anubis.
  • “In a real fight I won’t have time for you to heal me. You may not even be there at all!” Brutal irony in hindsight.
  • The vow: “stronger than strong and then stronger than that! Stronger than Orion, stronger than that glasses wearer and that horned asshole who’s always at the top of our class, stronger than Huoyan, and then even stronger than Anubis.” Two chapters later we meet “that glasses wearer” (Elias) and “that horned asshole” (Lucifer). He fights and beats both of them on TV in Section 3. The vow is a roadmap.
  • “They say my power is for rescue, support, and detaining. I say they’re right and wrong at the same time.” His power was classified as support. He refuses the label. Meanwhile, the girl next to him is proud to be a support hero.
  • “I can’t fail to save someone a second time!… by the time my training is done, you won’t even have a use for other heroes anymore! I’m gonna be able to handle it all on my own!” There’s the flaw: the lone hero who needs no one. He says this to the person who will die supporting him.
  • Abby closes her eyes and “could still see all his pain and suffering.” Her power has no off switch. That becomes a plot point in 2.5 (“My power scans on its own!”).
  • “She never thought anyone could be so comfortable in their own skin that one day they’d rock a maid outfit and the next they’d play the hero.” She admires his self-acceptance. He doesn’t have any. She’s seeing what she wants to see.
  • “One day, she hoped to find her own reason to keep moving forward.” Abby has no personal goal beyond helping. Lucifer calls that out in 2.1.
  • “Kage…I will be your number one supporter…” An echo of “number one hero,” and a promise she keeps with her life.

What it’s doing. It plants the whistle and the maid photo, gives Kage his explicit goal list, and turns Abby’s attachment from kindness into devotion.

What works. The robot gag is well-paced, the vow is a perfect roadmap, and Abby’s whisper at the end is one of the best chapter buttons in Section 1. Every important seed in this chapter pays off.

What I’d push on. Kage’s long speech about being a “jack of all trades” restates itself. “I can do all those things, but I can fight too! I can stop those villains before they even get the opportunity to hurt anyone innocent! I can’t fail to save someone a second time!” The last sentence is the real one. Consider cutting the first two and letting “I can’t fail to save someone a second time” land alone.

Score 8/10

1.6 The Race

2,321 words. Across the city, about two months later, a Saturday morning.

What happens. After two months of nonstop training, Kage spots Orion strolling past a ramen shop and ambushes him with tape. Kage challenges him to a race across the city. With a quarter-second head start, Kage launches himself on a two-story cement pillar (a new technique), flies on glue jets until his lipids run out, then swings on tape like “Arachnid Guy,” and rebounds off a bouncy glue blob. Mid-flight he names himself: first “Sticky Man” (Orion: “BRO, THAT NAME IS TERRIBLE!”), then “STICKY SITUATION.” Orion catches up in Zephyr Mode and wins. “Eat breakfast,” Orion tells him.

Details I caught.

  • “For the past two months.” The book jumps time cleanly and tells you so.
  • Orion strolling past a ramen shop at 8 a.m. with a backpack. He’s slow-walking again.
  • “Don’t make me blow a joint dodging your sneak attacks at eight in the morning.” “Blow a joint” is literal: his power strains his joints.
  • Orion’s nickname for Kage: “Speed-Limiter.” Kage’s for Orion: “Mercury.” Orion is “confused” by it and chalks it up to “Kage Antics.” In 1.15, we learn Kage meant it as a jab, and Orion corrects him: “I am Greek, Kage, not Roman.”
  • “Your power gives you superhuman reflexes too? C’mon, man, you’ve got a fucking cheat code…” Orion “stammered” and his “pulse quickened”: “It’s careful training, timing, and control.” Orion gets defensive about the idea that his speed is a gift. Inazuma presses the same bruise in 3.6: “You didn’t train for that speed! You just came out that fast.”
  • Orion’s “amber-gold eyes.”
  • “my speed isn’t just running. It’s movement in every direction.” He restates his 1.2 line. He means it.
  • “Just try to keep up, yeah?” Orion’s line, which Kage throws back at him (“Try and keep up!!!”). Orion: “Oh, you wanna use my line?” This is the first of many line-swaps between them.
  • “Get sticky, get sticky, get sticky…” Kage’s pre-fight mantra.
  • The quarter-second head start is smart: “your reaction time’s too fast if you’re the one saying go.”
  • The countdown is formatted as breaths: “He breathed. / ‘On your mark…’ / His fingers curled against the ground.” It’s cinematic. Abby’s countdown in 1.1 and Gene’s endless countdowns in Section 3 all echo this.
  • Cement. “A massive two-story pillar erupted from the street.” Kage can now make cement, and it becomes his signature (the gauntlets, the floating pillars).
  • “Right now…he was moving faster than a speedster.” A small, real triumph.
  • “Arachnid Guy or something.” A Spider-Man parody that dodges trademark trouble with a wink.
  • “Your friendly neighborhood, uh, Sticky Man coming through!” Orion: “BRO, THAT NAME IS TERRIBLE! PICK ANYTHING ELSE!” Remember this line. It comes back.
  • “IF YOU WANTED TO SWING, YOU SHOULD’VE TOLD ME! I WOULD’VE BROUGHT POPCORN!”
  • The bouncy glue: “It hardened, rubberized, and was most importantly, bouncy.” Kage makes a new adhesive mid-race. He’s inventive under pressure.
  • “This was a race, not a fight. He needed to calm down.” He stops himself from attacking. A tiny moment of restraint.
  • “STICKY SITUATION!!!” The hero name, and the book’s subtitle, born mid-air.
  • Orion: “…Sticky Situation. Yeah, that’s definitely getting shortened. I’m not yelling that in a crisis.” And then: “Name’s cool though. Kinda fits you.” He likes it and won’t admit it.
  • “Hey Sticky…” He stops himself. “No. Nope. Not saying that.”
  • “This is you turning into dragged luggage.”
  • “Anubis’s scouts are probably still laughing about ‘Sticky Situation.’” They really are watching. We see the photo with that caption in 1.14.
  • Kage: “I never wanna train with you! It’s more fun not knowing what you’ll do!” Narrator: “He only meant his first statement for about five seconds.” A perfect narrator wink.
  • “WHAT?! I LOST?! THAT’S BULLSHIT!”
  • Orion: “Also…eat breakfast. I saw you run out of juice back there.” This pays off in 3.11 with “I remembered to eat breakfast!”
  • “You improvise. I execute. That’s our combo.” The duo’s thesis. It returns in 2.6 (“You improvise, I execute!”) and 4.5 (“He improvised a plan for me to execute”).
  • “I’m still not saying your hero name. No way in a million years.” He says it in 2.6, in a crisis, to save both their lives.

What it’s doing. It’s the book’s first great set piece: a showcase of Kage’s creativity, the birth of his name, and the rivalry’s tender core.

What works. Nearly everything. The escalating techniques (pillar, jets, swinging, bouncing) are visual and fun. Every joke is a setup. The chapter has three lines that pay off sections later (“pick anything else,” “I’m not yelling that in a crisis,” “eat breakfast”), and each payoff is earned here.

What I’d push on. Orion’s voice in this chapter is noticeably cockier and wordier than in 1.1 (“I mean, I could give you a head start, but where’s the fun in that?”). It’s in range, since he loves competition, but a line acknowledging that racing brings out a side of him would make the shift feel deliberate. And the power-scaling question will occur to some readers: Kage keeps pace with a 200 mph speedster on glue jets. The text covers it (“not even trying”), but one more line of Orion visibly holding back would settle it.

Score 8.5/10

1.7 Class In Session

1,952 words. Huoyan’s classroom, the Monday after the race.

What happens. Abby heals a bruise on Kage’s ribs and kisses his cheek. Ailani ignores him. Rhatella and Orion arrive together, and Kage and Orion both announce “We’re not friends!” Kage explains their rivalry to Abby. Lucifer, Reina, and Elias arrive. Volkan arrives late and gets fifty laps. After class, Kage tries flirting with Reina, and Lucifer punches him, chokes him against a wall, and threatens to burn him alive. Huoyan, busy scolding Orion, misses it, then scolds Kage too: “HALF OF THE CITY WAS COVERED IN GLUE!” Anubis texted to spare them. Next time, no mercy.

Details I caught.

  • “It was a couple days after his race with Orion on Saturday.” That’s the race’s day, pinned.
  • Abby: “I saw it as soon as I walked in! Why do you think I sat next to you?”
  • The cheek kiss: “It’s not a problem, Kage! Anything for you!” Abby’s affection is open and unguarded. Kage blushes, then sees Ailani and “blushed harder.” His heart is elsewhere.
  • Rhatella walks in “hammering a piece of metal.” She and Orion are friends now, as promised.
  • Rhatella: “Hiss! It’s the Ozul kid!” She hisses. Rat.
  • “We’re not friends!” In unison, “in the same tone of voice.” Kage does an X with his arms; Orion fixes his dreads and cracks his knuckles. This is the motif’s first appearance, and every later “we’re not friends” depends on how funny this one is.
  • Rhatella: “Were you guys exes or something? C’MON TELL ME MORE!… I KILL FOR THIS RELATIONSHIP DRAMA!” She’s “gnawing on Orion’s arm.” Shipping and gnawing.
  • Kage’s explanation: “when me and Orion are in competition, we understand each other. We both have that strong desire to be great… He’s relaxed and I’m brash. He’s focused and I’m all over. He’s seven inches taller than me for god’s sake!” That height difference is a real number the book keeps.
  • “He could die tomorrow and I wouldn’t really grieve.” Eight hundred lines later, Orion thinks Kage did die, and grieves hard. Kage is wrong about both of them.
  • Abby: “Will I always have to pick one?” She’s already trying to hold the group together.
  • The trio: Reina (small, white hair, dark blue eyes, “strong posture”), Lucifer (tall, orange hair, light blue eyes, dark red horns and a tail), Elias (short, jet-black hair, glasses). Nicknames: “Lu,” “Luci,” “Min,” “Cruz.”
  • “You already know the answer is wherever Min sits.” Lucifer sits wherever Elias sits. That’s protective, and Section 4 explains why.
  • “The top second-year hero student in the world.” There’s only one program, so “in the world” is literally true. A dry joke.
  • Lucifer and Elias trade the top spot. “Elias, at least, was fairly humble.” Lucifer “came first in the entrance exam.”
  • “Abby’s eyes tracked Lucifer like a hawk. Her smile slipped, and she huffed.” On first read, it looks like dislike. After 2.1, you know she’s watching his condition worsen.
  • Abby: “you don’t have many friends do you?” Brutal, accurate, adorable.
  • Volkan: “a black hoodie,” “dark red eyes,” late. Huoyan: “Are you telling me that your routine is so elaborate and subject to change that you couldn’t come here when you’re supposed to?” Fifty laps: “Maybe counting those out will help you count your minutes in the morning.” Volkan’s surname is Turkish for “snail.” He’s late because he’s slow. The joke is buried in his name.
  • “The entire class sat up just a bit straighter in unison.” Good group beat.
  • Kage studies Lucifer: “What is he doing that I’m not?”
  • Kage to Reina: “why are you hanging out with these bums when you can go out with a stud like m-” He’s cut off by a fist. He flirts with Reina the week after begging Ailani for a second chance. That’s the flirt Lucifer later reports to Ailani.
  • Lucifer: “Listen to me, you sticky fucking freak.” Frostbite uses the exact same insult in 1.13. See Theories.
  • “You’re like an ant to me.” Lucifer’s insults are almost always insects (ant, bug, termite, worm, mosquito, cockroach). Abaddon’s are too.
  • “I’ll have no problem burning you alive.” He says the same thing to Abby in 2.1. It’s his stock threat, and he never carries it out.
  • Huoyan missed the assault because “Orion was standing right next to him.” Orion was being scolded first.
  • “HALF OF THE CITY WAS COVERED IN GLUE!” The race had consequences.
  • They both bow. Orion: “I didn’t stop him sir! I encouraged him, so I apologize too!” Orion takes half the blame. That’s integrity.
  • Huoyan “ran a hand through his hair to slick it back.” His tell when he’s angry.
  • “I have been dying to crack the whip on you two.”
  • “exchanging one quick glance—then splitting off in opposite directions.” The glance Kage misreads in the next chapter.

What it’s doing. It’s the cast chapter: it seats most of the class in one room, establishes the class hierarchy, and shows what Kage is up against socially.

What works. The “We’re not friends!” unison is perfect. Lucifer’s introduction is genuinely threatening. Huoyan’s scolding of Volkan is funny and establishes him as the strict teacher the class fears.

What I’d push on. This is a lot of new names for one chapter: Reina, Lucifer, Elias, Volkan, all at once. The trio’s introduction leans on a paragraph of exposition (“Kage wanted to audibly groan… He knew who they all were”). You could let Lucifer’s attack introduce him and save the background for later. Also, Huoyan missing a student being choked against a wall in his own classroom strains belief. One more line (he’s at the door, his back is turned, the class closes ranks) would help.

Score 7/10

1.8 Perfectionist Meets Wildcard

1,716 words. The training “city,” the same day.

What happens. Kage finds the training city, a mock town with burning buildings for rescue practice. The fire triggers a flashback, and he steadies himself. He picks a fight with a tall, monotone girl sitting quietly in the field. She repels him effortlessly. They bet the spot. Kage forms concrete gauntlets, gets thrown through a burning window, then shatters her barrier with a glue-launched dive, only for her to sidestep. She steps on his chest, admires his drive, and tells him drive doesn’t win fights. He asks her out. She laughs until she cries, tells him her name, Severine Neant, and says to come back when he’s “significantly superior.”

Details I caught.

  • “How dare he look at me like that when he knows damn well we’re not fighting today?!” He reads Orion’s glance as a challenge. It’s funny because it’s so Kage.
  • The training city: “purposefully damaged buildings for rescue practice. A few buildings were on fire.”
  • “That night, the broken windows, the awning’s destruction. He looked at his shaking hands. ‘It’s okay. This isn’t then. I’m better now…’” A brief, honest trauma response. I’d love more of these.
  • Severine: “five-foot-eleven, bluish-violet-haired,” “basic purple clothes,” “purple eyes.” She looks at Kage “and immediately turned away.”
  • Kage: “What makes you think you can be here and not train at all?” The boy who slept through a year lectures someone for resting.
  • Severine’s verbal tic: “You have now exhibited one of my dislikes: disruptions.” Then “incompetence.” In 3.2, “foolishness.” Her dislikes are a running list.
  • “Gosh, you are quite the charmer. That was almost hilarious, but let’s bring it down to just comical.” She’s funny, in a cold way.
  • Kage “threw his shoes and socks off.” Toenail glue. Consistent.
  • “I got a new technique just for you”: concrete gauntlets. He’ll use them against Abaddon until his wrists break.
  • Her card: Repulse-Redux. Repulsive fields, barriers, flight, shockwaves. Costs: stamina, weakens over time, concentration for multiple fields, recoil against powerful attacks.
  • “I strive for perfection above all else. If I lost to someone like you, that would be a disgusting blemish on my record.” She has never lost. Section 3 takes that away from her in front of millions.
  • The barrier shatters: “The light of the fire made each broken fragment in the air glitter. For a split second, he felt pride fill him completely.” Then “Severine just…sidestepped.” Lovely deflation.
  • She stands on his chest. In 1.15, Kage stands on Orion’s chest. He learns the pose from losing.
  • “Your determination—while very idiotic—is somewhat admirable. One day, in my quest for perfection, I hope to have your level of drive. But drive does not win fights.” That’s the truest thing anyone says to Kage in Section 1, and in Section 3, Kage beats Elias with strategy, not drive.
  • Kage, flat on his back: “you wouldn’t happen to be single, would you?” He flirts with Reina and Severine in the same week he begs Ailani. His flirting isn’t romance. It’s a reflex he uses to cover pain.
  • Severine laughs “harder than she had the whole time. Tears started to stream down her cheeks.” “I do not date fools, and certainly not those I believe to be weak.”
  • “Come to me when you are certain you are significantly superior to your current state.” This is the line she repeats over tea in 4.0.
  • “There is such a thing as a ‘sore winner,’ y’know?” “Do you wish for me to lie to you?”
  • “Great, another person on my ‘need to beat’ list. She’s at the back though. Orion is priority number one.”

What it’s doing. It introduces Severine, shows that Kage’s power alone won’t beat strategy, and gives the first on-page trauma flashback.

What works. Severine is a fully formed comic character in one scene, and she’s not just a joke. She sees something real in him. The barrier-shatter-sidestep beat is a perfect miniature of Section 1’s lesson: effort without strategy.

What I’d push on. Severine is introduced as someone who speaks without contractions (“I do not date fools,” “I am asking you to please leave”), but she slips (“you’re a riot,” “I’ve talked to you,” “you’re lucky”). Pick a rule and keep it. The no-contractions voice is distinctive, and it would make her later meltdown (“NO NO NO NO NO!”) even more of a break.

Score 7.5/10

1.9 Rhatella In A Novella

1,351 words. Rhatella’s lab, about two months later.

What happens. After two more months of losing, Kage goes looking for Rhatella’s support gear. C.O.R.E. scans him and draws his blood. When Kage interrupts its introduction (“You’re a robot. We get it.”), C.O.R.E. sighs and kicks him down a shaft with a giant boot. Rhatella, reading a romance novel, panics: his gear isn’t done. Kage explains why he chose the name “Sticky Situation,” and she’s won over. Orion arrives for their scheduled hangout, and the boys argue over who gets her. Rhatella, delighted, decides they’re secretly in love. Kage storms out.

Details I caught.

  • “Two more months of losses went by.” Another clean time jump. The total is now four months, which Huoyan confirms in 1.11.
  • The scans: “facial, retinal, and fingerprint scans. A syringe came in and stabbed Kage in the arm, drawing blood.” This time it works. “Hey! You better not sell that to anybody!” Rhatella’s lab now has a sample of Kage’s blood from before the biomass. Nobody has followed up on this.
  • C.O.R.E. is “even more emotionless than when talking to Orion.” It has opinions about people.
  • “C.O.R.E. audibly sighed despite it being an A.I.” Then “a massive boot kicked Kage through the entrance.” The AI has a temper. It’s one of the funniest images in Section 1.
  • Rhatella is “taking a moment to relax with a romance novel.” The title’s pun. It also explains her shipping brain.
  • “HISS-! HEY HEY! WHAT ARE YOU DOING HERE?! I’M NOT DONE WITH YOUR SUPPORT GEAR YET!” She aims a wrench at him.
  • “Your glue enhancement does not mesh well with my electricity.”
  • “I was born to be sticky! The name is: Sticky Situation!” Kage strikes “a funny-looking hero pose.”
  • Rhatella: “That name is awful! I could come up with a new one for you in seconds!”
  • The name speech. “It sounds like a name a kid would have fun yelling. It’s kinda like…a name of an action figure a kid could be playing with… Every time I save someone, I want to tell them my name and make them giggle. Just like that, the scary predicament becomes less terrifying. I can become a story people tell their families when they get home: ‘Can you believe the guy who saved me was named Sticky Situation?’ They’ll end up forgetting they were even in danger to begin with. A name that can turn something horrifying into something funny—that’s the kind of hero name others should aim for too.”
    • This is the most important speech in the book. It defines what Kage thinks a hero is for: not being seen, but making the rescued feel safe.
    • It’s also, quietly, about the store. The person under the awning didn’t get to forget. Kage wants to give other people the ending they didn’t get.
    • The “action figure” idea returns twice: Anubis has a single Anubis action figure on his office shelf (1.14), and Kage cuts his hair in 2.10 to look “just like an action figure!”
    • And it pays off in 3.16, when Kage sticks his tongue out at Alex in the middle of being destroyed, and Alex laughs.
  • “Hmm…I like that. Okay, Sticky Situation!” Rhatella is the first person to accept the name. That’s why Kage later calls himself “one of two or three people she can call her friends.”
  • “Recently I’ve been able to create cement!”
  • Orion: “Are we still hanging out today? C.O.R.E. was acting weird for some reason.” Their friendship has a schedule now.
  • “Of course you’re here. Just interrupt every aspect of my life, why don’t you?”
  • Kage: “I got here first! I get first dibs! That’s the rules!” Orion: “There are no rules, Kage!” In 1.1, Kage was the one who said “Rules?! C’mon, who needs ’em?!” They’ve swapped positions.
  • Orion: “You’re just here to get something out of her!” He’s protecting Rhatella from being used, the exact thing he warned her about in 1.4. He’s right, too. Kage came for gear.
  • Rhatella’s trance: “Two guys, one girl—my rizz factor’s fuckin’ updating!… What did my ugly ass even do to have two hotties arguing over my attention?!… they’re totally exploring each other’s bodies!… THEY NEED TO ELOPE IMMEDIATELY!” It’s funny, and the “ugly ass” line is a small knife. She thinks she’s ugly.
  • “Pain in the dick.” “I will gunk up this whole place!”
  • Orion’s “gold piercings shimmered in the light.”
  • “He’s just…Kage.”

What it’s doing. It gives Kage’s hero name its meaning, deepens Rhatella, and stages the first “two boys fight over a friend” comedy.

What works. The name speech, absolutely. It’s the reason the book is called what it’s called, and it’s earned by context: Kage says it to the one person who will listen. C.O.R.E.’s boot is a perfect sight gag. Rhatella’s shipping is a running joke that later turns poignant (“I wish I was the one they were all fighting over”).

What I’d push on. Rhatella’s shipping monologue is funny, but it’s long, and it runs to almost the same length as Kage’s name speech in the same chapter. Trim it by a third so the name speech stays the chapter’s center of gravity. Also, Kage leaves without his gear and doesn’t come back for it. In 2.10 he decides “Rhatella’s gear was something only the old Kage was after.” That’s a meaningful choice. It would land harder if, somewhere, he regretted not going back.

Score 8/10

1.10 Will Without A Way

1,444 words. The training field, Thursday after school.

What happens. Kage attends Huoyan’s optional Thursday class, which nobody ever attends. Huoyan laughs at the sight. Cheyenne Bandalore shows up too, thinking it’s mandatory because Huoyan wrote it on the board; he decides it’s now mandatory for her. Huoyan floats a fireball and explains that most enhancements (except body “mutations”) allow basic telekinesis over what you create. Kage tries to float a glue ball. Cheyenne tells him it’s fine if he can’t. He snaps at her. On his final attempt, his hair stands on end, the ball floats for a millisecond, and he collapses, convulsing. Huoyan, rattled, says simple telekinesis shouldn’t do that.

Details I caught.

  • The class is “optional, always had been,” and people believe “it was created strictly because the school forced Huoyan to have some after-school activity.” He enjoys “nobody ever showing up.”
  • Huoyan: “I must’ve fallen asleep in that chair! What a dream this is! Mr. Ozul has come to actually LEARN from me?… Are you trying to catch up to Tulivalo?” His favoritism shows immediately.
  • Kage, through gritted teeth: “Yes, Mr. Niao. I am here to train, so please help me become stronger.” Huoyan: “And the boy has learned respect too!”
  • Cheyenne’s introduction, the best costume paragraph in the book: “the soft metallic jingle of what sounded like either keys or pocket change… a blazer unbuttoned over a crisp white blouse and a red necktie that looked slightly loosened, topped with a crimson-and-gold serape draped over one shoulder and a cowboy hat she never took off… spurred cowboy boots, her shadowed eyes fixed on the teacher with the weary intensity of a desert hawk while she shifted a strawberry lollipop in her mouth like a smoldering cigar.” The serape and the cigar-lollipop are a Clint Eastwood “Man with No Name” homage. The strawberry lollipop turns the gunslinger into a teenager.
  • That jingle: “keys or pocket change.” Her surname, Bandalore, is an old English word for a yo-yo. Remember that.
  • Kage, internally: “Why is she here? I was hoping to get a one-on-one session…”
  • “Two in one day. That has to be a record.” Huoyan is enjoying himself.
  • “Kage was unaware Huoyan had any personality outside of…dictator.”
